690.4pt}}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{6})=\mathfrak{osp}\left(V_{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.33772pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.16885pt\hbox{\kern 0.33772pt\vbox to2.0266pt{\vss\hbox to1.68887pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.0266pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt}\kern-0.16885pt\kern-2.0266pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt}&\lower 0.33772pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.16885pt\hbox{\kern 0.33772pt\vbox to2.0266pt{\vss\hbox to1.68887pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.0266pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt}\kern-0.16885pt\kern-2.0266pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt}&\lower 0.33772pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.16885pt\hbox{\kern 0.33772pt\vbox to2.0266pt{\vss\hbox to1.68887pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.0266pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt}\kern-0.16885pt\kern-2.0266pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt}\cr\lower 0.33772pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.16885pt\hbox{\kern 0.33772pt\vbox to2.0266pt{\vss\hbox to1.68887pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.0266pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt}\kern-0.16885pt\kern-2.0266pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt}&\lower 0.33772pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.16885pt\hbox{\kern 0.33772pt\vbox to2.0266pt{\vss\hbox to1.68887pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.0266pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt}\kern-0.16885pt\kern-2.0266pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt}\cr\lower 0.33772pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.16885pt\hbox{\kern 0.33772pt\vbox to2.0266pt{\vss\hbox to1.68887pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-2.0266pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\vrule height=2.0266pt,width=0.33772pt}\kern-0.16885pt\kern-2.0266pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt\kern 1.68887pt\hrule width=2.36432pt,height=0.33772pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}\right).

Consolidating Marin’s results for the image of each irreducible representation we
finally arrive at:

Lemma 21

(Marin)
.

The images of the irreducible representations associated with
\lambda\vdash\ell
of

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\ell}
are as follows:

\rho_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\ell})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathfrak{sl}(V_{[\ell-1,1]}),&\lambda=[\ell-r,1^{r}],0<r<\ell-1,\\
\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),&\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}\text{ proper},\\
\mathfrak{osp}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),&\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\text{ proper},\end{array}\right.

where this Lie algebra isomorphism is an equality except when

\lambda=[\ell-r,1^{r}]
for
r>1
.

2.2.4
Semisimple decomposition of the algebra of transpositions

Marin proceeds to use results summarized in the previous sections to explicitly
decompose
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{|\lambda|}
as a direct sum of simple algebras
(Theorem
23
below).
In the course of this proof,
the following useful fact is derived
[
MARIN2007742
, Section 6.6]
:

Lemma 22

(Marin)
.

Given distinct partitions
\lambda,\mu\vdash\ell
such that
\dim(\boldsymbol{\lambda})>1
and

\dim(\boldsymbol{\mu})>1
,
the map given for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\ell}
by
\rho_{\lambda}(x)\mapsto\rho_{\mu}(x)

is a Lie algebra isomorphism
if and only if

\mu=\lambda^{\prime}
or
\lambda
and
\mu
are both hooks.

Below we state one of the main theorems of
[
MARIN2007742
]
, that being an isomorphism
between
\mathfrak{g}’_{\ell}
and the direct sum of
its simple ideals, in the form of a commuting diagram which indicates how the isomorphism was constructed. In a series of commuting diagrams, Marin illustrated how
the image of each irreducible representation
\rho_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\ell})

relates to the image of the sum over all partitions of the associated
irreducible representations, ultimately demonstrating how a subset of such irreducible
representations uniquely determines the image of the full sum.
The purpose in doing so, accepting the hypothesis that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\ell}
is isomorphic to
a direct sum of such images, was to determine
which representations need constitute that sum.
Here we compose those commuting diagrams together
[
MARIN2007742
, Theorem A]
:

Theorem 23

(Marin)
.

The following diagram of Lie algebra homomorphisms commutes:

\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{\lambda\vdash\ell}\mathfrak{gl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{\lambda\vdash\ell}\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}\end{subarray}}(\text{Id}_{\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\oplus\tilde{M}_{\lambda})\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\text{Id}_{\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{osp}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\ell}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\rho_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{\lambda\vdash\ell}\rho_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{sl}(V_{[\ell-1,1]})\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}\\
\text{ proper}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\\
\text{ proper}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{osp}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{r=1}^{\ell-2}\Delta_{r}\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}\\
\text{ proper}\end{subarray}}\text{Id}_{\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\\
\text{ proper}\end{subarray}}\text{Id}_{\mathfrak{osp}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}}
\scriptstyle{\left({\bigoplus\limits_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\rho_{\lambda}}\right)^{-1}}

where
\Lambda\equiv\{[\ell-1,1]\}\cup\{\lambda\vdash\ell|\lambda_{2}>1,\lambda\leq\lambda^{\prime}\}
,
\iota
denotes inclusion,
\tilde{M}_{\lambda}
is the automorphism
of
\mathfrak{gl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
defined by
X\mapsto-M_{\lambda}X^{\intercal}M_{\lambda}^{-1}
,
and

\Delta_{r}:\mathfrak{sl}(V_{[\ell-1,1]})\rightarrow\mathfrak{sl}(V_{[\ell-r,1^{r}]})

is defined such that

\Delta_{r}(X)(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{r})=Xv_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{r}+v_{1}\wedge Xv_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{r}+v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge Xv_{r}

for all
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\in V_{[\ell-1,1]}
.

To review the maps depicted in the diagram, starting from the bottom right,
the first map determines all hook representations from wedge products of
[\ell-1,1]

via the isomorphisms
\Delta_{r}
, the second map is an inclusion map indicating that
self-conjugate representations are confined to the subalgebra
\mathfrak{osp}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
of

\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
, the third map determines the conjugate representation for each
non-self-conjugate proper partition via the isomorphism induced by the alternating
intertwiner
M_{\lambda}
, and the fourth map is another inclusion map indicating that
representations of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\ell}
necessarily map to traceless elements of
\mathfrak{gl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
.
Also note that, in the isomorphism

\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\rho_{\lambda}:\mathfrak{g}_{\ell}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathfrak{sl}(V_{[\ell-1,1]})\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}\\
\text{ proper}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\\
\text{ proper}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{osp}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),

by Lemma
22
the hook in
[\ell-1,1]
in
\Lambda
can be replaced by any other hook

[\ell-r,1^{r}]
of the same size
\ell
, and also any partition can
be replaced by its conjugate. Thus

\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\rho_{\lambda}\right)(\mathfrak{g}_{\ell}^{\prime})\cong\mathfrak{sl}(V_{[\ell-1,1]})\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}\\
\text{ proper}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{sl}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell\\
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\\
\text{ proper}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{osp}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),

where
\Lambda
can be generalized such that it contains any one hook, one of each
conjugate pair of proper partitions, and all proper self-conjugate partitions.

The above isomorphism implies an important corollary regarding the interdependency of
the representations. Using Lemma
21
the isomorphism can be written

\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\rho_{\lambda}\right)(\mathfrak{g}_{\ell}^{\prime})\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\rho_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{g}_{\ell}^{\prime}).

As this remains true when we remove partitions from both sides, we obtain a slightly
more general statement:

Corollary 24
.

Given
\Lambda\subset\{\lambda\vdash\ell\}
, we have

\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\rho_{\lambda}\right)(\mathfrak{g}_{\ell}^{\prime})\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}(\rho_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{g}_{\ell}^{\prime}))
if and only if
\Lambda
is free of nontrivial
conjugate pairs and multiple hooks.

Trivial representations are not a concern since
\rho_{[\ell]}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\ell})=\rho_{[1^{\ell}]}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{\ell})=0
.
By further application of Lemma
22
, conjugates and hooks can be added to the sums on both sides
of the isomorphism of Corollary
24
, as follows.

Corollary 25
.

Given a set
\mathcal{P}
of any partitions of
n
, we have

\left(\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{hooks}\\
\nu\in\mathcal{P}\end{subarray}}\rho_{\nu}\right)(\mathfrak{g}_{n})\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{conjugate pairs}\\
\nu\neq\nu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\end{subarray}}(\rho_{\nu}\oplus\rho_{\nu^{\prime}})(\mathfrak{g}_{n})\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{rest of}\\
\nu\in\mathcal{P}\end{subarray}}\rho_{\nu}(\mathfrak{g}_{n})\cong\left(\bigoplus_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}}\rho_{\nu}\right)(\mathfrak{g}_{n}).

2.3
Relevant intertwiners

As representations of the symmetric group
and unitary group are critical to this work, maps that are equivariant
with respect to such actions are also of primary concern. We use the terms

G
-intertwiner
or
G
-module homomorphism
interchangeably for linear maps
that are equivariant with respect to action of the group
G
, and sometimes indicate
such a map with
G
above the arrow: “
\overset{G}{\rightarrow}
”, or isomorphism symbol: “
\overset{G}{\cong}
”. The space of such maps
between vector spaces
V
and
W
is itself a vector space, denoted
\Hom_{G}(V,W)
.
Typically we are interested in equivariance with respect to a Cartesian product of
symmetric groups, irreducibly represented by the first argument of
\Hom
, and when this
equivariance
is understood the group subscript is suppressed. The material for this section is
primarily drawn from Fulton and Harris
[
fulton1991representation
]
, Goodman and Wallach
[
goodman1998representations
]
, and Harrow

[
10.5555/1195346
]
.

2.3.1
Schur–Weyl duality

A fundamental intertwiner which makes exchange-only encoding possible is called Schur–Weyl duality. For our purposes, it may be stated as an
S_{\ell}\times\text{SU}(d)
-module isomorphism between the Hilbert space
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes\ell}
and a certain sum of
irreducible
S_{\ell}\times\text{SU}(d)
-representations.
More commonly, and more generally, it is expressed in terms of an arbitrary vector
space
V
as a
S_{\ell}\times\text{GL}(V)
-module isomorphism, as follows
[
fulton1991representation
, Exercise 6.30]
:

V^{\otimes\ell}\overset{S_{\ell}\times\text{GL}(V)}{\cong}\bigoplus_{\lambda\vdash\ell,\lambda^{\prime}\,_{1}\leq d}V_{\lambda}\otimes U^{(d)}_{\lambda}.

Here,
U^{(d)}_{\lambda}
is the irreducible representation of
GL(V)
,
or “Weyl module”, canonically
associated with Young diagram
\lambda

[
fulton1991representation
, Section 6.1]
,
\text{GL}(V)
acts on
V^{\otimes\ell}
by
simultaneous multiplication on every vector
v_{i}\in V
of the product
v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\ell}\in V^{\otimes\ell}
, and
S_{\ell}
acts on
V^{\otimes\ell}
by permuting the vectors in the product

v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\ell}
.

In this regard, it may be clarifying to remark that every finite dimensional
irreducible representation of
\text{GL}(d,\mathbb{C})
restricts to an irreducible representation of

\text{SU}(d)
.
In fact, referring to Fig. (
2.1
), the restriction of an irreducible
representation of any of the depicted groups to any of the indicated subgroups
(connected by a line segment in the figure) is also irreducible.
An irreducible representation of
\text{GL}(d,\mathbb{C})

restricted to
\text{SL}(d,\mathbb{C})
remains irreducible because each element of the former is a
scalar multiple of an element of the latter
[
fulton1991representation
, p.222–223]
.
By a similar argument, an irreducible representation of
\text{U}(d)
restricted to
\text{SU}(d)

likewise remains irreducible.
Furthermore, an irreducible representation of
\text{GL}(d,\mathbb{C})
or

\text{SL}(d,\mathbb{C})
restricted to
\text{U}(d)
or
\text{SU}(d)
, respectively, remains irreducible by an argument
due to Weyl known as the “unitary trick”
[
fulton1991representation
, Section 9.3]
. In brief,
in such a case when complexification of the Lie algebra of the subgroup generates the
original group, it can be deduced that reducibility of a representation restricted to the
former would imply reducibility of the representation of the latter.

\textstyle{\text{GL}(d,\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\text{U}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\text{SL}(d,\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\text{SU}(d)}

Figure 2.1:
Subgroups of the general linear group
\text{GL}(d)
that share irreducible representations.

So basis vectors of irreducible representations of both
\text{GL}(d,\mathbb{C})
and
\text{SU}(d)

can be taken to be the Weyl tableaux described in Section 2.1.1. As an example
of the use of both Weyl tableaux and Young tableaux in the context of Schur–Weyl duality,
consider

(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 3}\overset{S_{3}\times SU(2)}{\cong}(V_{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to1.91325pt{\vss\hbox to1.59442pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-1.91325pt\vrule height=1.91325pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 1.59442pt\vrule height=1.91325pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-1.91325pt\hrule width=2.23209pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 1.59442pt\hrule width=2.23209pt,height=0.31883pt}&\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern 0.15941pt\hbox{\kern 0.31883pt\vbox to1.91325pt{\vss\hbox to1.59442pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-1.91325pt\vrule height=1.91325pt,width=0.31883pt\kern 1.59442pt\vrule height=1.91325pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-1.91325pt\hrule width=2.23209pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 1.59442pt\hrule width=2.23209pt,height=0.31883pt}&\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern 0.00002pt\kern height=1.91325pt,width=0.31883pt}\kern-0.15941pt\kern-1.91325pt\hrule width=2.23209pt,height=0.31883pt\kern 1.59442pt\hrule width=2.23209pt,height=0.31883pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}\otimes U^{(2)}_{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.31883pt\vbox{\kern .

2.3.2
Inducing from a product of symmetric group representations

Another relevant intertwiner is that between a tensor product of symmetric group
representations, representing the Cartesian product of their symmetric groups,
and the induced representation of the symmetric group containing the factor groups.
For the tensor product of two irreducible representations, this induced representation
is given by the formula
[
fulton1991representation
, Equation 4.41]

Ind
_S_—
λ
—×S_—
μ
—^S_—
λ
—+—
μ
—
λ
⊗
μ
=⨁_
ν
⊢—
λ
—+—
μ
—c_
λμ
^
ν
ν
,

where the symbol
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}
denotes the Littlewood–Richardson coefficient,
which here gives the multiplicity of each irreducible representation
\boldsymbol{\nu}
in
the representation induced from
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu}
.
For the tensor product
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu}

above, and henceforth for products of symmetric group
representations, it is assumed that the group
acting on the resulting module is the Cartesian product of the groups acting on
each factor module.
The above formula may be
considered a definition of the Littlewood–Richardson coefficient, although it has
many other equally fundamental applications, some of which will be demonstrated shortly.
We also define it in Section 4.1 in terms of how it is calculated.

As Fulton and Harris remark
[
fulton1991representation
, p.58]
, the above operation of taking a tensor product of
representations and inducing a new representation can be generalized for a tensor
product of arbitrarily many representations. Since inducing representations is
transitive
[
fulton1991representation
, Exercise 3.16]
, the above formula immediately generalizes by iteration:

Lemma 26
.

Given a family of partitions
\{\mu^{(i)}\vdash m_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
, and
n=\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}
, we have

\text{Ind}_{S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{k}}}^{S_{n}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(1)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(k)}}=\bigoplus_{\nu\vdash n}c_{\mu^{(1)}\mu^{(2)}}^{\nu^{(2)}}c_{\nu^{(2)}\mu^{(3)}}^{\nu^{(3)}}\cdots c_{\nu^{(k-2)}\mu^{(k-1)}}^{\nu^{(k-1)}}c_{\nu^{(k-1)}\mu^{(k)}}^{\nu}\boldsymbol{\nu},

where the Einstein summation convention is assumed, so there is an implicit sum
over each repeated index.

The above operation of tensor multiplication
and representation induction is both commutative and associative
[
fulton1991representation
, p.58]
,
in the sense that the direct sum over
\nu
of vector spaces
\boldsymbol{\nu}
, including their
multiplicities, is
independent of the ordering of the tensor
product and of the particular Littlewood–Richardson coefficients that result.
Therefore we can unambiguously define the coefficient

c_
μ
^(1)⋯
μ
^(k)^
ν
≡c_
μ
^(1)
μ
^(2)^
ν
^(2)c_
ν
^(2)
μ
^(3)^
ν
^(3)⋯c_
ν
^(k-2)
μ
^(k-1)^
ν
^(k-1)c_
ν
^(k-1)
μ
^(k)^
ν

with the understanding that its value is invariant with respect to the order of its lower indices.

2.3.3
Restricting to a product of symmetric group representations

The Littlewood–Richardson coefficient also gives the multiplicity of the product

\boldsymbol{\lambda}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu}
in the restriction of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
from
S_{|\lambda|+|\mu|}
to

S_{|\lambda|}\times S_{|\mu|}

[
fulton1991representation
, Exercise 4.43]
. By Schur’s lemma this can be
expressed as
[
fulton1991representation
, p.16]

dim(Hom(

Res

^S_—
λ
—+—
μ
—_S_—
λ
—×S_—
μ
—
ν
,
λ
⊗
μ
))=c_
λμ
^
ν
.

As with induced representations this formula can be generalized for products of
arbitrarily many representations. In fact the latter may be derived from the former
by Frobenius reciprocity:

Lemma 27
.

The multiplicity of
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(k)}
in the restriction of

\boldsymbol{\nu}
from
S_{n}
to

S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{k}}
is given by
c_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(k)}}^{\nu}
.

Proof.

Using Frobenius reciprocity in the form
\Hom_{G}(V,\text{Ind}_{H}^{G}W)\cong\Hom_{H}(V,W)
, we have

\displaystyle\Hom_{S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{k}}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{\nu})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Hom_{S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots S_{m_{k}}}(\boldsymbol{\nu},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(k)}),

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Hom_{S_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\nu},\text{Ind}_{S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{k}}}^{S_{n}}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(k)}),

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Hom_{S_{n}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu},\bigoplus_{\nu}c_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(k)}}^{\nu}\boldsymbol{\nu}\right).

Hence we have

\dim(\Hom_{S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{k}}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{\nu}))=c_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(k)}}^{\nu}.

∎

2.3.4
Isotypical decomposition

Lemma
27
implies that the maximum number of pairwise orthogonal subspaces of

\boldsymbol{\nu}
that are
S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{N}}
-module isomorphic to

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}
is
c_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}
.
We call the direct sum
of a maximal number of orthogonal such subspaces a
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}
-isotypical subspace,
denoted by
V_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}
.
Alternatively,
V_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}
may be defined as the span of all subspaces of
\boldsymbol{\nu}

that are
S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{N}}
-module isomorphic to

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}
, which by Lemma
27
must have a total dimension
of
\prod_{i=1}^{N}\dim(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)})c_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}
.
Of course, many such isotypical subspaces
typically comprise
\boldsymbol{\nu}
. Since by Maschke’s Theorem the restriction of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
to
S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{N}}
can, like any finite group representation, be decomposed into irreducible representations,
we have
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\bigoplus_{\mu^{(i)}\vdash m_{i}}V_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}
.
The orthogonality of these isotypical subspaces comes as a consequence of Schur’s lemma, and can be
stated more precisely as follows
[
panyushev
, Lemma I.5.3]
:

Lemma 28
.

Given isotypical subspaces
V_{\lambda^{(1)}\cdots\lambda^{(N)}}^{\nu}
and
V_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}
of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
,
if
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(i)}
and
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}

are inequivalent representations of
S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{N}}

then
V_{\lambda^{(1)}\cdots\lambda^{(N)}}^{\nu}
and
V_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}
are orthogonal with respect to any

S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{N}}
-invariant inner product on
\boldsymbol{\nu}
.

Note that there exists a canonical map
\chi_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}
onto each isotypical subspace
V_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}
from
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}\otimes\Hom(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\nu})
, given by

\chi_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}(\psi\otimes\iota)=\iota(\psi)
.
Considering
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}\otimes\Hom(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\nu})
as an

S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{N}}
-module such that

s.(\psi\otimes\iota)=(s.\psi)\otimes\iota
, it follows that
[
goodman1998representations
, Proposition 3.1.6]

Lemma 29
.

The linear map

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}\otimes\Hom(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\nu})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle V_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}

\displaystyle\psi\otimes\iota

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\iota(\psi)

gives an
S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{N}}
-module isomorphism.

For the purpose of studying operators on the irreducible components of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
,
it is often convenient to use the above isomorphism to consider the
corresponding operators on

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}\otimes\Hom(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\nu})
.

It is useful to note that
\chi_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}
is an orthogonal transformation with respect
to
S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{N}}
-invariant inner products naturally defined on its domain and range, as
follows. We already defined the Euclidean inner product applicable to

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}
in terms of tableau bases.
An inner product is then naturally induced on
\Hom(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\nu})
, up to scaling, by the condition that intertwiners are
orthogonal if and only if their images are orthogonal.
The scaling is then fixed by the further condition that

\langle\iota,\iota\rangle=1
if and only if

\iota:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\nu}
is an isometry.
An inner product is then defined on
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}\otimes\Hom(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\nu})
by factoring:

\langle\psi\otimes\iota,\psi^{\prime}\otimes\iota^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\psi,\psi^{\prime}\rangle\langle\iota,\iota^{\prime}\rangle
.
It follows that
\langle\psi\otimes\iota,\psi^{\prime}\otimes\iota^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\iota(\psi),\iota^{\prime}(\psi^{\prime})\rangle
,
and then immediately that:

Lemma 30
.

The canonical isomorphism

\chi_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}\otimes\Hom(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\nu})\rightarrow V_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}

is an isometry with respect to the inner product defined above on

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)}\otimes\Hom(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\nu})

and the Euclidean inner product on the tableau basis of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
.

2.3.5
Isomorphism between a sum and product of unitary group representations

Combining the isotypical decomposition of symmetric group representations with
Schur–Weyl duality leads to an important intertwiner with respect to the special
unitary group. Following Harrow
[
10.5555/1195346
, Claim 7.1]
, by Schur–Weyl duality we have

\displaystyle(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes(\ell+m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes\ell}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes m}

\displaystyle\overset{S_{\ell}\times S_{m}\times\text{SU}(d)}{\cong}

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell,\mu\vdash m\\
\lambda^{\prime}\,_{1}\leq d,\mu^{\prime}\,_{1}\leq d\end{subarray}}(V_{\lambda}\otimes U^{(d)}_{\lambda})\otimes(V_{\mu}\otimes U^{(d)}_{\mu}).

By Schur–Weyl duality and Lemma
27
we also have

\displaystyle(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes(\ell+m)}

\displaystyle\overset{S_{\ell+m}\times\text{SU}(d)}{\cong}

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\vdash\ell+m\\
\nu^{\prime}\,_{1}\leq d\end{subarray}}(V_{\nu}\otimes U^{(d)}_{\nu})

\displaystyle\overset{S_{\ell}\times S_{m}\times\text{SU}(d)}{\cong}

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash\ell,\mu\vdash m,\nu\vdash\ell+m\\
\lambda^{\prime}\,_{1}\leq d,\mu^{\prime}\,_{1}\leq d,\nu^{\prime}\,_{1}\leq d\end{subarray}}c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mu}\otimes U^{(d)}_{\nu}.

Consistency then demands

⨁_

U^(d)_

.

See
[

, Section 6.1]
or
[

for more discussion and proofs of the above isomorphism.
As with the symmetric group representations this formula can be iterated for products
of arbitrarily many representations, yielding:

Lemma 31
.

Given
n=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\mu^{(i)}|
, we have

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}U^{(d)}_{\mu^{(i)}}\overset{\text{SU}(d)}{\cong}\bigoplus_{\nu\vdash n}c_{\mu^{(1)}\cdots\mu^{(N)}}^{\nu}U^{(d)}_{\nu}.

For the purpose of describing physical implementations of exchange-only
quantum computers, we are interested in an
S_{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{m_{N}}\times\text{SU}(d)
-embedding of the subspace

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}(V_{\mu^{(i)}}\otimes U^{(d)}_{\mu^{(i)}})
of the Schur–Weyl decomposition of the product of

2.4
Littlewood–Richardson rules

Having introduced the Littlewood–Richardson coefficients by their applications to
representation theory, we now explain how to calculate them, and give some useful
relationships with the partitions upon which their values depend.
It should be noted that the applications of Littlewood–Richardson
coefficients are many and varied
[
2020arXiv200404995B
]
.
The century-spanning literature pertinent to calculating or constraining their values
is also quite rich.
Here we only summarize the key results needed for our present purposes,
but some references given below may provide a glimpse of this greater mathematical vista.

2.4.1
Definitions

The approach to calculating the Littlewood–Richardson coefficients given below is
consistent with that originally introduced by Littlewood and Richardson
[
LR
]

and elaborated upon in
[
fulton1991representation
]
and elsewhere.
There are many alternative methods, which may have relative advantages.
But the traditional approach given below is sufficient for our purposes.

To define the Littlewood–Richardson coefficients it is convenient to introduce the Littlewood–Richardson tableau. First, we require some more basic concepts:

Definition 32
.

Given Young diagrams
\nu
and
\lambda

such that
\nu_{i}\geq\lambda_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
the skew diagram denoted by

\nu\backslash\lambda
is obtained by the removal of the
first
\nu_{i}
cells from the
i
th row of
\lambda
, for all
i
, with the remaining
cells of
\nu
left in place.

Definition 33
.

A Littlewood–Richardson sequence is a sequence of natural numbers,
with repetition, such that within any initial truncation of the sequence a given number

j
appears at least as often as any number greater than
j
.

Definition 34
.

A Littlewood–Richardson tableau is a skew, semistandard tableau
such that when read from right to
left and top to bottom the entries form a Littlewood–Richardson sequence.

The following examples clarify what a Littlewood–Richardson tableau is and is not.

Example 35
.

The skew tableau

\scriptstyle 1

\scriptstyle 2

\scriptstyle 2

is a Littlewood–Richardson tableau. It is in fact the only Littlewood–Richardson tableau of this shape with three 1’s and two 2’s.

Example 36
.

The skew tableau

\scriptstyle 1

\scriptstyle 2

\scriptstyle 1

is not a Littlewood–Richardson tableau because
1,2,2,1,1
is not a Littlewood–Richardson sequence.

Example 37
.

The skew tableau

\scriptstyle 1

\scriptstyle 1

\scriptstyle 2

is not a Littlewood–Richardson tableau because it is not semistandard.

Before proceeding to the Littlewood–Richardson coefficients we
require another definition:

Definition 38
.

The
{\it weight}
of a semistandard tableau is a partition, the
k
th part
of which equals the number of times
k
appears in the tableau.

Finally, the following definition encapsulates the Littlewood–Richardson rules.

Definition 39
.

Given partitions
\lambda
,
\mu
, and
\nu
,
a Littlewood–Richardson coefficient
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}
is the number
of Littlewood–Richardson tableaux of shape
\nu\backslash\lambda
and weight
\mu
.

2.4.2
Identities

The Littlewood–Richardson coefficients satisfy a number of symmetries
[
2014arXiv1409.8356G
]
. Two symmetries that we need are as follows:

Lemma 40
.

Given partitions
\lambda
,
\mu
, and
\nu
, we have

c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}=c_{\mu\lambda}^{\nu}=c_{\lambda^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}^{\nu^{\prime}}
.

These symmetries can be demonstrated by
algebraic applications
of the Littlewood–Richardson coefficients
[
2014arXiv1409.8356G
]
.
That
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}=c_{\mu\lambda}^{\nu}
follows from the observation
that the operation of taking the exterior tensor product of
\lambda
with
\mu
, and from it inducing
a representation of
S_{|\lambda|+|\mu|}
, is commutative
[
fulton1991representation
, p.58]
. That
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}=c_{\lambda^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}^{\nu^{\prime}}
follows from the invariance
of Schur functions with respect to the involutive algebra automorphism that transforms
the associated partition into its conjugate
[
HANLON19921
]
.

Yet the Littlewood–Richardson rules themselves are not manifestly symmetric in the ways
indicated by Lemma
40
, and proving these symmetries directly by such a combinatorical approach is notoriously nontrivial
[
2014arXiv1409.8356G
]
.
Nevertheless it can be done.
The symmetry
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}=c_{\mu\lambda}^{\nu}
is painstakingly shown to arise from Littlewood–Richardson tableaux in
[
2016arXiv160305037A
]
,
and
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}=c_{\lambda^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}^{\nu^{\prime}}
is proved via the observation
that there are always as many Littlewood–Richardson tableaux of shape
(\nu\backslash\lambda)^{\prime}
and weight
\mu^{\prime}
as there are Littlewood–Richardson tableaux of shape

\nu\backslash\lambda
and weight
\mu
in
[
HANLON19921
]
.

A useful albeit obvious identity that follows immediately from the preceding results is that each
row of
\nu
must be greater than that of
\lambda
or
\mu
whenever
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}>0
:

Lemma 41
.

Given partitions
\lambda
,
\mu
, and
\nu
,
if
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}>0
then we have
\nu_{i}\geq\max(\lambda_{i},\mu_{i})
, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By definition of nonzero
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}
, the skew diagram
\nu\backslash\lambda
exists so
\nu_{i}\geq\lambda_{i}
for all
i
.
Further, by symmetry of the Littlewood–Richardson coefficient,
Lemma
40
, we have
c_{\mu\lambda}^{\nu}=c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}
,
so
\nu_{i}\geq\mu_{i}
for all
i
. Thus we have
\nu_{i}\geq\max(\lambda_{i},\mu_{i})
.
∎

Finally, another well-known consequence of the Littlewood–Richardson rules comes
in handy for the part-wise sum of partitions,

\lambda+\mu\equiv[\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}+\mu_{k},\ldots]

[
fulton_1996
, Chapter 5, Exercise 2]
:

Lemma 42
.

Given any partitions
\lambda
and
\mu
, it follows that
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\lambda+\mu}=1
.

Proof.

By definition, each row of
(\lambda+\mu)\backslash\lambda
is the same length
as that of
\mu
. Therefore, a Littlewood–Richardson tableau of weight
\mu

can be constructed by filling the
i
th row of
(\lambda+\mu)\backslash\lambda
with

\mu_{i}
occurrences of
i
, so
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\lambda+\mu}>0
.
That this is the only Littlewood–Richardson tableau of shape
(\lambda+\mu)\backslash\lambda
and weight
\mu

can be proved inductively.
The 1st row of
(\lambda+\mu)\backslash\lambda
must be filled with

\mu_{1}
occurrences of 1, because if it is not then the higher numbers
that must appear in the rightmost cells to satisfy the definition of a
semistandard tableau violate the Littlewood–Richardson sequence.
For some
k\in\mathbb{N}
and each
1\leq i\leq k
, assume that the
i
th row of

(\lambda+\mu)\backslash\lambda
is filled with
\mu_{i}
occurrences of
i
.
Then the
(k+1)
th row of
(\lambda+\mu)\backslash\lambda
must be filled with

\mu_{k+1}
occurrences of
k+1
, because if it is not then the higher numbers
that must appear in the rightmost cells to satisfy the definition of a
semistandard tableau again violate the Littlewood–Richardson sequence.
∎

2.4.3
Horn inequalities

In 1962 Horn conjectured that, given
d\times d
Hermitian matrices
A
and
B
with
eigenvalues
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
and
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
respectively,

A+B
has eigenvalues
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}

if and only if the latter satisfy
a certain set of
inequalities with
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
and
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}

[
Horn:conj
]
.
Horn’s conjecture was finally proved in the affirmative by Knutson and Tao in 1999
[
1998math......7160K
]
.
The reason this concerns us here is because, in the process of proving Horn’s conjecture,
Knutson and Tao built on earlier work by Klyachko establishing a close relationship between Hermitian eigenvalues and Littlewood–Richardson coefficients
[
30002131808
]
. In particular we have
[
1999math......8012F
,
anderson_richmond_yong_2013
]

Lemma 43

(Klyachko–Knutson–Tao)
.

Given partitions
\lambda
,
\mu
, and
\nu\vdash|\lambda|+|\mu|

with at most
d
rows each, we have

c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}>0
if and only if there exist

d\times d
Hermitian matrices
A
and
B
such that

A
,
B
, and
A+B
have eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
,

\{\mu_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
and
\{\nu_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}

respectively.

This result alone is quite powerful, since there are cases where
constructing the requisite Hermitian matrices with the desired eigenvalues may be easier than
direct application of the Littlewood–Richardson rules, as we demonstrate in Section 4.2.4.

In conjuction with Knutson and Tao’s proof of Horn’s conjecture, then,
we have the machinery of the Horn inequalities at our disposal.
Applied to the Littlewood–Richardson coefficients, this takes the following form
[
1999math......8012F
, Theorem 11]
:
Given partitions
\lambda
,
\mu
, and
\nu\vdash|\lambda|+|\mu|
with at most

d
parts each, we have
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}>0
if and only if

∑_k∈K
ν
_k≤∑_i∈I
λ
_i+∑_j∈J
μ
_j

for all
(I,J,K)
in a set of triples of subsets of
\{1,\ldots,d\}
, as
prescribed in
[
FULTON200023
, Theorem 1]
.
Or equivalently
[
1999math......8012F
, Equation (7)]
, we have

∑_i∈I^c
λ
_i+∑_j∈J^c
μ
_j≤∑_k∈K^c
ν
_k

where
I^{c}
,
J^{c}
, and
K^{c}
are the complements of
I
,
J
, and
K
in

\{1,\ldots,d\}
.

In the present work however we will use only a subset of the Horn inequalities,
ones that conveniently constrain individual parts of
\nu
rather than sums of parts. Originally discovered by Weyl in the context of Hermitian eigenvalues

[
Weyl1912
]
, one such set of inequalities takes the form
[
Fulton1997-1998
]
:

ν
_i+j-1≤
λ
_i+
μ
_j, i+j-1≤d.

A related set of inequalities, derivable from the Weyl inequalities and
referred to as their dual
[
taoblog
]
, may be considered a subset of
the complement of the Horn inequalities above
[
1999math......8012F
, Equation (11)]
:

λ
_i+
μ
_j≤
ν
_i+j-d, i+j-d≤d.

Setting
k=i+j-1
, the Weyl inequalities may be expressed

ν
_k≤min_i+j=k+1{
λ
_i+
μ
_j},

while setting
k=i+j-d
, the dual Weyl inequalities may be expressed

max_i+j=k+d{
λ
_i+
μ
_j}≤
ν
_k.

In light of Lemma
43
, the above results can be put in the
following form:

Lemma 44

(Weyl)
.

Given partitions
\lambda
,
\mu
, and
\nu
,
if
c_{\lambda\mu}^{\nu}>0
then for all
1\leq k\leq d=\nu^{\prime}\,_{1}
, we have

\max_{i+j=k+d}\{\lambda_{i}+\mu_{j}\}\leq\nu_{k}\leq\min_{i+j=k+1}\{\lambda_{i}+\mu_{j}\},

where as indicated the maximum on the left hand side is over the set
\{(i,j)|1\leq i,j\leq d,i+j=k+d\}
and the minimum on the right hand side is over the set
\{(i,j)|1\leq i,j\leq d,i+j=k+1\}
.

Chapter 3
Exchange-only quantum computing

The purpose of this chapter is to provide some physics context for mathematicians,
and perhaps vice-versa.
Our specific aim is to introduce those quantum computing concepts needed to motivate
relevant mathematical problems. Broader context can be found, for example, in
[
mikeandike
]
.
For the purposes of the present work, quantum computation consists primarily of two kinds of
components: “qudits”, those being the fundamental units of quantum information that generalize
binary digits, and logical gates, those being the fundamental steps in a computation.

A
d
-dimensional qudit may be indentified with a
d
-dimensional Hilbert space.
We also consider each qudit as a
d
-dimensional
G
-module for a group
G
, that is as a group representation.
Multiple qudits can then be combined by taking the tensor product.
For example,
n
qudits of
d
dimensions each can be expressed as
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
.
Of course as a Hilbert space this product is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{d^{n}}
. What distinguishes
the two expressions is the set of operators we consider on the product, as determined by
the tensor product of the component
G
-modules, which naturally respect the qudit structure.

A gate is a unitary operator on one or more qudits.
If the qudits are denoted by
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(1)}\ldots\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(n)}
,
the relevant gates are elements of
\text{SU}(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(n)})
.
We focus on the special unitary group, rather than the unitary group, because
elements of the latter differ by the former only by scalar “phase” factors which
typically have no bearing on computations.
The reason for the irrelevance of such scalar factors is that, strictly speaking,
quantum physical states, and thus the associated logical states, are given by rays in a projective Hilbert space.
Of vital importance, however, is that every element of the relevant special unitary group be
implementable on the given qudits, using whatever operations are permitted by the underlying physical system. We call this essential property “universality”, and developing its mathematical
definition occupies much of this and the next chapter.

We could instead ask for just “approximate universality”
[
mikeandike
]
, meaning the physically implementable
operators are dense in the desired unitary operators. But in our case, the implementable operators are
generated by a representation of a Lie algebra,
which proves to be isomorphic to the special linear, special orthogonal, or symplectic algebra.
The implementable operators thus form a matrix Lie group
G
, which has the
property that if any sequence of its elements converges to a matrix
A
,
then
A
is either in
G
or is not invertible
[
Hall
]
. Since every unitary operator
is invertible, we conclude in our case that approximate universality implies exact
universality, and so we need only consider the latter.

3.1
Logical qudits

Often a qudit can be identified with a subspace of a Hilbert space corresponding to a quantum physical
system, as indicated above. Such a qudit is sometimes called a “physical qudit”, and textbook examples include the spin of
a particle such as an electron. Mathematically, a
d
-dimensional physical qudit is also commonly identified with the
defining representation of
\text{SU}(d)
.

More generally, a qudit need not be identified with a subspace of such a Hilbert space, but rather
with a “subsystem” of the Hilbert space. A subsystem refers to a tensor factor of a
subspace of a Hilbert space: if
\mathcal{H}=\bigoplus_{i}S_{i}\otimes B_{i}
, then each factor space
S_{i}
or
B_{i}
is a subsystem
[
DFS
]
.
A measurement of the state of such a qudit, say
S_{i}
, would then be associated with a “logical observable” of the
form
O\otimes\mathbb{1}
, where
O
is a Hermitian operator corresponding to an observable of interest and
\mathbb{1}
is the
identity on
B_{i}
. A logical gate, however, is a unitary operator of the form
U\otimes A
, where

U
is the gate of interest and
A
is arbitrary.

3.1.1
Noiseless subsystems

While in principle any subsystem of the Hilbert space may serve as a qudit, some choices are better than others.
For example, there are typically many physical sources of noise which may introduce errors into the encoded information.
Ideally, then, a subsystem can be chosen on which some such noise acts as the identity. That is, if
\mathcal{H}=\bigoplus_{i}S_{i}\otimes B_{i}
, and the noise acts as
\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{1}\otimes N_{i}
, then any
S_{i}
is a good choice of qudit.
Such a subsystem, or qudit, is called “noiseless” or “decoherence-free”
[
DFS
]
.

A common type of noise is called “collective” noise
[
PhysRevA.63.042307
]
. In this context, the relevant Hilbert space is assumed to be a product
of
d
-dimensional physical qudits, which in order to distinguish from logical qudits we refer to as “
d
-state systems”.
Collective noise is noise that affects many
d
-state systems at the same time, in the same way. Typically, collective noise
is further assumed to act as an operator in
\text{SU}(d)
. For example, if the
d
-state systems are particles with spin,
collective noise might simultaneously flip all the spins. This could be the case if the source of noise is electromagnetic
radiation with wavelength long compared to the distance between the particles. Therefore, we are motivated to find a
collective-decoherence-free subsystem (CDFS).

Finding every physically relevant CDFS in a Hilbert space is readily achieved by Schur–Weyl decomposition.
Given
\ell
copies of the
d
-state system
V
, the resulting Hilbert space is

V^⊗ℓ

S

ℓ

×
SU
(d)

≅

⨁_
λ
⊢ℓ,
λ
’ _1≤dV_
λ
⊗U^(d)_
λ
.

As first proposed by Zanardi and Rasetti
[
Zanardi
]
,
each
V_{\lambda}
can be used as a collective-decoherence-free logical qudit, which we call a CDFS qudit.
Operating on
d
-state systems by a swap then corresponds to acting on
V_{\lambda}
by a transposition.
Collective noise meanwhile acts on the
d
-state systems by simultaneous matrix multiplication,
and on
U_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\lambda}
as a nontrivial
operation on
U_{\lambda}
times the identity on
V_{\lambda}
.

It is assumed that the only operations on the
d
-state systems utilized for computation on the CDFS qudits
are those generated by quantum physical exchange-interactions, by which the states of

d
-state systems are interchanged. The exchange-interactions may be identified with transpositions
permuting a set of numbers labeling the
d
-state systems.
Exchange-interactions among the
d
-state systems comprising a single logical qudit then generate
gates on this logical qudit, and exchange-interactions among
d
-state systems of multiple logical
qudits generate gates on these combined logical qudits.
In addition to the protection it provides from collective noise, a CDFS qudit may also be a convenient choice for ease of control in physical systems where the exchange interaction is readily implemented.

3.1.2
Physical and logical bases

Thinking in terms of certain bases may help clarify the above concepts, as well as bridge the gap to
common physics formulations when considering practical applications. For this purpose we use
Dirac’s bra-ket notation, whereby a vector labeled by
v
is denoted by the “ket”
\ket{v}
.
A basis of a logical qudit may be
identified with the quantum analogs of logical values in a many-valued logic: 0, 1, and (possibly)
so forth. The associated basis vectors are denoted
\ket{0_L}
,
\ket{1_L}
,
\ket{2_L}
, up to

\ket{(D-1)_L}
for a
D
-dimensional qudit. Since the logical qudits at issue are symmetric group
representations, which already have a convenient basis in the form of Young tableaux, it is natural
to identify the logical basis vectors with the Young tableaux. This identification is
consistent with literature on the topic, for example
[
PhysRevA.99.042331
]
.

Physical qudit, or
d
-state system, basis vectors may be similarly labeled, without the
L
subscript. Tensor products of such
basis vectors, e.g.
\ket{1}\otimes\ket{0}\otimes\ket{2}
, are commonly denoted by a string of such labels in a
single ket, e.g.
\ket{102}
. These form a basis of the Hilbert space consisting of a product of such

d
-state sytems. So the
d^{n}
basis vectors of the Hilbert space
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes\ell}
have the form

\ket{b_1\cdots b_\ell}
where
b_{i}\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
. We call this the physical basis.

Since the Hilbert space
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes\ell}
is also a representation of
S_{\ell}\times\text{SU}(d)
, each
physical basis vector can be acted upon by elements of
S_{\ell}\times\text{SU}(d)
. In particular,
transpositions act on physical basis vectors by transposing
d
-state system labels:

(i j)

—

b_1⋯b_i⋯b_j⋯b_ℓ

⟩

=

—

b_1⋯b_j⋯b_i⋯b_ℓ

⟩

.

By Schur--Weyl duality, each tensor product of a Young tableau in
V_{\lambda}
with a Weyl
tableau in
U^{(d)}_{\lambda}
maps by
S_{\ell}\times\text{SU}(d)
-intertwiner to a linear combination of
physical basis vectors in
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes\ell}
which is unique up to overall scalar factor.

In the examples below we consider 2-state systems and,
in order to make contact with relevant physics literature,
further associate the 2-state systems with spin-1/2 particles such as electrons.
For the purpose of illustrating the correspondence between the mathematical
and physical ideas,
an operator of particular interest on the Hilbert space
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes\ell}
is
the
z
-component of the collective spin operator,
S_{z}
.
By convention,
S_{z}
acts on the Hilbert space as a diagonal operator in the physical basis,
for which each eigenvalue equals half the number of 0’s labeling the basis vector minus
half the number of 1’s labeling the basis vector. So for example

S_{z}\ket{010}=\frac{1}{2}\ket{010}
and
S_{z}\ket{111}=-\frac{3}{2}\ket{111}
.
By further convention
S_{z}
is proportional, by a factor of
\frac{i}{2}
,
to an element in the fundamental representation of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
.
Therefore by Schur–Weyl duality
S_{z}
acts trivially on
V_{\lambda}
,
for each
\lambda\vdash\ell
,
and nontrivially on
U_{\lambda}
.
For each such
\lambda
,
S_{z}
is also diagonal in the Weyl tableau
basis, for which each eigenvalue equals one half the number of 1’s in the
tableau minus one half
the number of 2’s in the tableau.

So for example
S_{z}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower and

height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}
.
Another physical quantity of note is the collective spin magnitude
S
,
which for a given
U_{\lambda}
equals the maximum eigenvalue of
S_{z}
.
It follows that a collective spin magnitude is uniquely associated with each

U_{\lambda}
by the equation
S=\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})
.

Our goal is to map tableaux to physical basis vectors, in order to provide insight into noiseless
subsystems, as well as to make connections with potential physical applications.
We consider three examples of noiseless subsystems: two logical qubits and a logical qutrit.
The existence of all three of these encodings have been previously noted
[
PhysRevA.63.042307
]
.
The two logical qubits have also been given previously in terms of the explicit
Hilbert space basis used below, and in fact have been rather extensivey
investigated
[
divincenzo:qc2000b
,
PhysRevA.99.042331
,
Hsieh2003
]
. In contrast,
the logical qutrit and higher dimensional qudits have not received
as much attention in the literature, but have potential advantages suggested in the next subsection.

Example 45
.

Consider encoding a logical qubit on three 2-state systems. The Schur–Weyl decomposition of
the corresponding Hilbert space is:
